·3· · · · · · ·MR. BURCH: Would the room please come

·4· ·to order?

·5· · · · · · ·MR. BUTLER:· Thank you.· Good morning

·6· ·and welcome.· The Office of the United States

·7· ·Trade Representative, in conjunction with the

·8· ·Interagency Section 301 Committee, is holding

·9· ·this public hearing in connection with the

10· ·Section 301 investigation of Brazil's acts,

11· ·policies, and practices related to digital trade

12· ·and electronic payment services, unfair

13· ·preferential tariffs, anti-corruption

14· ·enforcement, intellectual property protection,

15· ·ethanol market access, and illegal

16· ·deforestation, initiated in accordance with the

17· ·specific direction of the President on July 15,

18· ·2025.

19· · · · · · ·On June 1, 2026, the Trade

20· ·Representative determined that certain of

21· ·Brazil's acts, policies, and practices related

22· ·to digital trade and electronic payment


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                     7

·1· ·services, unfair preferential tariffs, anti-

·2· ·corruption enforcement, intellectual property

·3· ·protection, ethanol market access, and illegal

·4· ·deforestation are unreasonable or discriminatory

·5· ·and burden or restrict U.S. commerce and are

·6· ·therefore actionable under Section 301 of the

·7· ·Trade Act of 1974.· Detailed information

·8· ·concerning this investigation is set out in the

·9· ·Federal Register notices published on July 18,

10· ·2025 at 90 Fed. Reg. 34069 and June 4, 2026 at

11· ·91 Fed. Reg. 33854.· The notices are also

12· ·available on the USTR web page.

13· · · · · · ·The purpose of this hearing is to

14· ·receive public testimony regarding the proposed

15· ·action to be taken in the investigation.

16· ·Consistent with the rules and procedures set

17· ·forth in the June 4, 2026 Federal Register

18· ·notice, witnesses have provided USTR and the

19· ·Section 301 Committee with a summary of their

20· ·testimony in advance of today's hearing.· The

21· ·Section 301 Committee will carefully consider

22· ·today's testimony, as well as all written

   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f

·1· ·comments in response to the Federal Register

·2· ·Notice of Determination.· The Section 301

·3· ·Committee will then make a recommendation to the

·4· ·Trade Representative regarding the final action

·5· ·to be taken in this investigation.

·6· · · · · · ·Before we proceed with testimony, I

·7· ·will provide some procedural and administrative

·8· ·instructions.· Please note that no cameras or

·9· ·video or audio recording will be allowed during

10· ·the hearing.· The hearing is scheduled for two

·1· · · · · · ·We have 14 panels of witnesses in total

·2· ·with 80 individuals scheduled to testify. The

·3· ·provisional schedule has been posted on the USTR

·4· ·website.· Today we will proceed with panels 1

·5· ·through 7 and then we will continue tomorrow

·6· ·with panels 8 through 14.· We will have a brief

·7· ·break between panels, a one-hour break for

·8· ·lunch, and, if time allows, a brief break in the

·9· ·afternoon.

10· · · · · · ·Each witness appearing at the hearing

11· ·is limited to five minutes of oral testimony.

12· ·The light before you will be green when you

13· ·start your testimony, yellow means you have one

14· ·minute left, and red means your time has

15· ·expired.

16· · · · · · ·After the testimony from each panel of

17· ·witnesses, the Section 301 Committee will have

18· ·an opportunity to ask questions.· All questions

19· ·will be from agency representatives.· There will

20· ·be no questions accepted from the floor.

21· ·Committee representatives will generally direct

22· ·their questions to one or more specific

·1· ·witnesses.· We will not be accepting post-

·1· ·a little bit closer.

·2· · · · · · ·MS. SILVA:· -- Association,

·3· ·representing 68 member companies that account

·4· ·for over half of Brazil's milled rice market.

·5· ·Because a 25-percent tariff on Brazilian

·6· ·processed rice would immediately disrupt U.S.

·7· ·supply chains and harm consumers, ABIARROZ

·8· ·respectfully requests the exclusion of long-

·9· ·grain processed rice under tariff codes Heading

10· ·1006 from the proposed additional duties.

11· · · · · · ·Tariff-free access is vital for U.S.

12· ·small businesses and regional supply chains.

13· ·The proposed tariff would disrupt their

14· ·operations, also affecting logistics, storage,

15· ·transportation, and ports, while increasing

16· ·dependence on concentrated Asian sources.· So my

17· ·remarks focus on three points: regional supply

18· ·diversification, the operational impact on U.S.

19· ·businesses, and consumer protection against

20· ·artificial inflation.

21· · · · · · ·Based on U.S. trade and tariff data,

22· ·for the last five years, the United States needs


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                    13

·1· ·7.7 million metric tons of rice annually for

·2· ·domestic and export commitments.· With domestic

·3· ·production averaging 6.3 million tons, a natural

·4· ·gap of 1.3 million tons makes imports essential

·5· ·for food security and export agreements.

·6· · · · · · ·Brazil is the third-largest supplier

·7· ·for long-grain variety, with a 5.5-percent

·8· ·market share, providing niche distributors and

·9· ·private-label programs, which are the main

10· ·market for our product, with premium, uniform,

11· ·nonstick rice.· This supply is vital because 78

12· ·percent of U.S. long-grain imports are highly

13· ·concentrated in only two sources, Thailand and

14· ·India.· Relying on this duopoly exposes U.S.

15· ·companies to trans-oceanic disruptions and

16· ·volatile export quotas.

17· · · · · · ·In contrast, Brazil provides a stable,

18· ·market-driven alternative in the Western

19· ·Hemisphere.· Logistically, regional shipping

20· ·from Brazil avoids maritime choke points, taking

21· ·half the transit time of trans-Pacific voyages.

22· · This tariff will not boost domestic production.


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                    14

·1· · It will simply penalize a close geographic

·2· ·partner and force U.S. buyers deeper into Asian

·3· ·dependencies.

·4· · · · · · ·Operating on narrow profit margins,

·5· ·these small businesses cannot absorb a sudden

·6· ·25-percent cost shock or product change

·7· ·expenses.· This tariff acts as an immediate

·8· ·operational hazard, forcing companies to pass

·9· ·higher costs directly to American consumers.

10· · · · · · ·Data proves that additional duties

11· ·introduce artificial rigidities into basic food

12· ·staples.· USDA reports show that during the 2025

13· ·cycle, global prices dropped by 22.24 percent

14· ·due to an international surplus and India

15· ·lifting export limits.· Normally, this brings

16· ·retail relief.· However, BLS data shows that

17· ·tariffs insulate domestic retail prices from

18· ·global corrections, keeping prices artificially

19· ·high and worsening the public perception of food

20· ·inflation.

21· · · · · · ·In conclusion, excluding these specific

22· ·long grain lines supports the U.S. food network,


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                    15

·1· ·safeguards small businesses and protects

·2· ·consumers.· Therefore, I respectfully request

·3· ·the USTR to grant a formal exclusion for these

·4· ·three subheadings under tariff code Heading 1006

·5· ·for long grain brown, polished, and parboiled

·6· ·rice.· Thank you for your time and

·7· ·consideration.

·8· · · · · · ·MR. BUTLER:· Thank you.· Mr. Bitting.

·9· · · · · · ·MR. BITTING:· Good morning.· My name is

10· ·Mark Bitting.· I am the president of Gehring

11· ·Montgomery, a small distributor in Warminster,

12· ·Pennsylvania, right outside of Philadelphia.

13· ·Thank you for the opportunity to appear today.

14· ·I'm here to support the administration's

15· ·decision to exempt carnauba wax imported from

16· ·Brazil from the proposed Section 301 tariffs and

17· ·to elaborate on why maintaining this tariff

18· ·exemption should continue in perpetuity.

19· · · · · · ·Carnauba wax is a natural vegetable wax

20· ·harvest from the leaves of the carnauba which

21· ·only grows commercially in northeast Brazil,

22· ·nowhere else on the earth.· There is no domestic


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                       16

·1· ·production of carnauba wax in the United States.

·2· · The palm does not grow here in any commercial

·3· ·way, and there is no U.S. industry that would be

·4· ·protected by imposing tariff.· Tariff on

·5· ·carnauba wax would therefore not encourage

·6· ·American production.· It would simply become a

·7· ·tax on the American people, manufacturers,

·8· ·farmers, food packagers, pharmaceutical

·9· ·companies, retailers, and ultimately consumers.

10· · · · · · ·Our company, Gehring Montgomery,

11· ·supplies carnauba wax to the entire United

12· ·States across food processing, agriculture,

13· ·pharmaceuticals, cosmetics, personal care, and

14· ·industrial manufacturing.· We are asking that

15· ·the United States maintain the tariff exemption

16· ·for this natural agricultural raw material,

17· ·which cannot be produced in this country and is

18· ·a critical raw material used for American

19· ·agriculture, food preservation, and regulated

20· ·consumer products.

21· · · · · · ·The most important point I want to make

22· ·today is that carnauba wax is not a luxury item.


                          Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433              Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                    17

·1· · It is a plant-based renewable material that has

·2· ·long been accepted for the food grade

·3· ·applications in the United States.· Carnauba wax

·4· ·is a functional and necessary agricultural

·5· ·material.· The U.S. Food and Drug Administration

·6· ·recognizes carnauba wax as generally safe for

·7· ·use in food for human consumption.

·8· · · · · · ·One of the most important uses in

·9· ·edible coatings are for fruits and vegetables,

10· ·including apples, citrus, cucumbers, peppers,

11· ·avocados, and other produce sold throughout the

12· ·United States.· These coatings help reduce

13· ·moisture loss, preserve appearance, extend shelf

14· ·life, and reduce spoilage during transportation

15· ·and storage.· In a country where food costs and

16· ·food waste are already serious concerns, making

17· ·this material more expensive would move us in

18· ·the wrong direction.

19· · · · · · ·It is also used as an inactive

20· ·ingredient for pharmaceutical tablets, where it

21· ·can help polish, coat, protect, and support the

22· ·handling of finished dosage forms.· If the cost


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                    18

·1· ·of carnauba wax is increased, companies would

·2· ·not simply absorb the cost forever.· Some will

·3· ·pass through to customers.· Others will look for

·4· ·lower cost synthetic or artificial alternatives.

·5· · In my view, that would be poor public policy.

·6· · · · · · ·Carnauba wax is a natural, proven

·7· ·material with a long history of safe and

·8· ·effective use.· Tariffs on carnauba wax could

·9· ·push the U.S. market toward substitutes that may

10· ·not have the same natural origin, acceptance,

11· ·performance, and consumer confidence.

12· · · · · · ·It is also important to understand that

13· ·carnauba wax has unique performances.· The

14· ·economic impact would reach far beyond the U.S.

15· ·importers.· They are already managing higher

16· ·costs for labor, freight, energy, insurance, and

17· ·compliance.

18· · · · · · ·There is also the issue of consistency

19· ·in tariff treatment.· In prior communications

20· ·with the Department of Agriculture and the Trade

21· ·Commission, I explained that carnauba wax

22· ·warrants tariff treatment consistent with other


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                     19

·1· ·similar situated agriculture wax products.· For

·2· ·these reasons, I respectfully request that the

·3· ·Section 301 Committee maintain the exemption

·4· ·provided for carnauba wax.

·5· · · · · · ·In closing, a tariff on carnauba wax

·6· ·would not punish a foreign competitor in favor

·7· ·of an American producer because no American

·8· ·producer exists.

·1· ·coerced labor, and corruption, which are not

·2· ·isolated abuses, but functioning business models

·3· ·that no U.S. rancher can or would ever want to

·4· ·compete with.

·5· · · · · · ·Second, cattle laundering and hollow

·6· ·traceability promises.· As testified in our 2023

·7· ·testimony to the Senate Finance Committee,

·8· ·cattle laundering is the practice of moving

·9· ·cattle through a series of intermediary ranches

10· ·and feedlots to scrub the paper trail.· The

11· ·monitoring systems used by Brazil's big

12· ·exporters are designed around that gap.· They

13· ·focus on direct suppliers and largely ignore the

14· ·indirect ones, where the dirt actually lives.

15· ·This is not a technical limitation, it is an

16· ·intentional decision.· Now, Brazil says, don't

17· ·worry, we're going to fix this with a mandatory

18· ·comprehensive traceability system by 2032.· From

19· ·a U.S. cattle producer's perspective fighting to

20· ·stay in business today, simply taking Brazil at

21· ·its word and waiting another six years is not a

22· ·remedy, it's a betrayal of trust.

·1· · · · · · ·Third, all Brazilian bovine products,

13· ·parasitic varroa mites, and require chemical

14· ·treatments.

15· · · · · · ·Conversely, Brazil utilizes Africanized

16· ·bees, while they are highly aggressive, these

17· ·bees are naturally immune to varroa mites and

18· ·other diseases, and allows for completely

19· ·chemical-free beekeeping.· Strict USDA organic

20· ·certification requires vast forage areas free

21· ·from chemical pesticides.

22· · · · · · ·Due to extensive U.S. commercial


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                    150

·1· ·agriculture, finding compliant organic forage

·2· ·domestically is nearly impossible.· Brazil

·3· ·offers the vast, isolated, and organic compliant

·4· ·landscapes required to meet this standard.

·5· ·Brazilian organic honey occupies a specialized

·6· ·niche segment of the U.S. market.

·7· · · · · · ·Reliable global supply is strictly

·8· ·limited, and Brazilian producers are among the

·9· ·few capable of meeting the volume, quality, and

10· ·traceability standards required by U.S. food

11· ·manufacturers and retailers.· Applying

12· ·additional tariffs to this product will not

13· ·advance the objectives of this investigation.

14· · · · · · ·Instead, it will cause unintended harm.

15· · This specialized product cannot be readily

16· ·replaced by domestic or alternative foreign

17· ·supply.· The honey industry operates on razor

18· ·thin margins, importers and packers simply

19· ·cannot absorb the high tariffs.· As we observed

20· ·this last year, these added costs are passed

21· ·directly to American consumers, who demand

22· ·organic choices.


                        Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433            Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                     151

·1· · · · · · ·An exemption for Brazilian organic

·2· ·honey aligns perfectly with the Committee's

·3· ·guidelines to exclude products that are

·4· ·difficult to source elsewhere, or whose

·5· ·inclusion would cause disproportionate supply

·6· ·chain disruptions.· Because organic honey is a

·7· ·narrowly defined agricultural product with its

·8· ·own exclusive HTS code, 0409.00.0005, this

·9· ·exemption can be easily and precisely

10· ·administered without affecting other segments of

11· ·the honey market.

13· ·preserve consumer access to healthy

14· ·alternatives, and avoid unnecessary supply chain

16· ·exclude certified organic honey under HTS code

17· ·0409.00.0005 of Brazilian origin from the final

18· ·Section 301 tariff action.

·9· ·firms.

11· ·when a central bank regulates and also operates

12· ·a major payment system, how do we make sure the

13· ·system is fair?· That is a governance question,

14· ·it should be addressed with clear rules, not

15· ·broad tariffs.· This is why I propose a digital

16· ·payment neutrality test.

18· ·fair access, interoperability, clear rules, good

19· ·data, governance, strong security, and financial

20· ·integrity.· In simple words, it should be open,

21· ·safe, transparent, and useful for private

22· ·innovation.· PIX is a major public innovation.

·7· ·that's--about the theory of indirect land use

·8· ·change or induced land use change, but a lot of

·9· ·it is predicated on the fact that if you

10· ·increase ethanol production in the US, that you

11· ·are somehow clearing land or deforesting in

12· ·Brazil.· And so we are being charged about

13· ·what's occurring in Brazil and frankly, we have

14· ·argued about the value all the time.· And at the

15· ·same time, you have others who are saying that

16· ·we should have a zero ILUC penalty.· And Matt

17· ·has talked about what our farmers are able to do

18· ·when the rules are fair.· Us being charged a

19· ·penalty for land use change that occurs in

20· ·Brazil, it just makes little to no sense,

21· ·especially for a state low carbon fuel program

22· ·within the United States.
